    *   If $z_C = z_D = 0$ (relative to A), $A$ is origin.
    *   $C = (2, 0, 0)$. $D = (1/2, \sqrt{3}/2, 0)$.
    *   $B$ is on z-axis, say $(0,0,1)$.
    *   Plane $ABD$ contains $(0,0,0), (0,0,1), (1/2, \sqrt{3}/2, 0)$.
    *   This is the plane spanned by z-axis and vector $(1/2, \sqrt{3}/2, 0)$.
    *   Normal is $(-\sqrt{3}/2, 1/2, 0)$.
    *   Vector $CD = D - C = (-3/2, \sqrt{3}/2, 0)$.
    *   Dot product: $(-3/2)(-\sqrt{3}/2) + (\sqrt{3}/2)(1/2) + 0 = \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{4} + \frac{\sqrt{3}}{4} = \sqrt{3} \ne 0$.
    *   **WAIT.**
    *   I checked if $CD$ is *parallel* to normal.
    *   Parallel means $CD \perp$ plane.
    *   Parallel condition: $\vec{CD} = k \vec{n}$.
    *   $\vec{CD} = (-1.5, 0.866, 0)$.
    *   $\vec{n} = (-0.866, 0.5, 0)$.
    *   Ratio x: $-1.5 / -0.866 = 1.5 / 0.866 = 1.5 / (\sqrt{3}/2) = 3/\sqrt{3} = \sqrt{3}$.
    *   Ratio y: $0.866 / 0.5 = (\sqrt{3}/2) / (1/2) = \sqrt{3}$.
    *   Ratios are equal.
    *   So vectors are parallel.
    *   So $CD$ is perpendicular to plane.
    *   My dot product check was for perpendicularity *between* CD and normal? No.
    *   If $CD \perp$ plane, then $CD \parallel$ normal.
    *   Dot product of parallel vectors is non-zero (unless zero vector).
    *   I calculated dot product $\vec{CD} \cdot \vec{n}$.
    *   $\vec{CD} \cdot \vec{n} = |\vec{CD}| |\vec{n}| \cos(0) = |\vec{CD}| |\vec{n}| \ne 0$.
    *   So dot product being non-zero is expected.
    *   So C is indeed correct.

    *   **Let's check B again. Maybe there's a trick.**
    *   $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$.
    *   Is it possible to interpret "distance to line AB" differently?
    *   No.
    *   Is it possible "dihedral angle" implies something else?
    *   Maybe $C$ and $D$ are on the *same* plane? No, angle 60.
    *   Maybe the distance $CD$ can be smaller if $C$ and $D$ are on opposite sides of the axis?
    *   If angle is 60, $C$ at 0, $D$ at 60.
    *   Distance $\sqrt{3}$.
    *   If $D$ is at -60 (angle 300), distance $\sqrt{3}$.
    *   If $D$ is at 120? Angle between planes is 60.
    *   Plane of $D$ is at 60 or -60?
    *   Plane is a full plane.
    *   Plane at angle 60 contains rays 60 and 240.
    *   $D$ must be in this plane.
    *   Distance from axis is 1.
    *   So $D$ can be at angle 60 or 240.
    *   If $D$ at 240 (-120).
    *   $C$ at 0.
    *   Angle between 0 and 240 is 120?
    *   Wait, dihedral angle is angle between planes.
    *   Plane 1 (xz) and Plane 2 (angle 60).
    *   Angle is 60.
    *   Points in Plane 2 can be at angle 60 or 240.
    *   If $D$ is at 240, its coordinates are $(-1/2, -\sqrt{3}/2)$.
    *   Distance to $C(2, 0)$ is $\sqrt{(2.5)^2 + (-\sqrt{3}/2)^2} = \sqrt{6.25 + 0.75} = \sqrt{7}$.
    *   So minimum is still $\sqrt{3}$.
    *   So B holds.

    *   **Is it possible that Option B is "CD $\ge 2$"?**
    *   No, $\sqrt{3} \approx 1.732 < 2$.
    *   Maybe I calculated distance wrong?
    *   $C(2,0)$, $D(1/2, \sqrt{3}/2)$.
    *   $x$ diff 1.5. $y$ diff $\sqrt{3}/2 \approx 0.866$.
    *   $1.5^2 = 2.25$.
    *   $0.866^2 = 0.75$.
    *   Sum = 3.
    *   Sqrt = 1.732.
    *   Calculation correct.

    *   **Let's rethink the problem statement.**
    *   "A, B fixed points".
    *   Maybe $C, D$ are constrained to be on the segment $AB$?
    *   "Moving point C to line AB distance is 2".
    *   If $C$ was on segment $AB$, distance would be 0.
    *   So $C$ is not on line.
    *   Maybe $C$ and $D$ are in a plane containing $AB$?
    *   No, dihedral angle 60.
    *   Maybe "moving point C" means $C$ moves, but for a specific instance?
    *   The question asks "Then ( )".
    *   This implies a deduction from the setup.
    *   Usually, in Chinese math problems, if there's a conditional option like C, and an unconditional inequality like B, and both seem true, one might be "more" true or I missed a constraint.
    *   But mathematically, if B is true, it's a valid option.
    *   Unless... "CD" refers to something else? No.
    *   Unless... "distance to line AB" means shortest distance? Yes.
    *   Unless... A and B are specific points? No.

    *   **Let's check if C is *always* true.**
    *   "When $AB \perp CD$".
    *   Is it possible that $AB \perp CD$ implies something else?
    *   We found it implies $z_C = z_D$.
    *   And we found the conclusion follows.
    *   Is there any edge case?
    *   What if $C$ and $D$ are the same point?
    *   Distance to line 2 and 1. Impossible.
    *   What if $C$ projects to $A$?
    *   Then $C$ is in plane perp to $AB$ at $A$.
    *   $z_C = 0$ (if A is origin).
    *   If $D$ also projects to $A$, $z_D = 0$.
    *   Then $AB \perp CD$ holds.
    *   Then $CD \perp$ plane $ABD$?
    *   Plane $ABD$ contains $A, B, D$.
    *   If $D$ projects to $A$, $D$ is in plane perp to $AB$ at $A$.
    *   So $AD \perp AB$.
    *   Plane $ABD$ is the plane containing $AB$ and $AD$.
    *   Since $AD \perp AB$, this plane is well defined.
    *   $C$ is also in plane perp to $AB$ at $A$.
    *   So $AC \perp AB$.
    *   $C, D$ are in the same plane (perp to $AB$ at $A$).
    *   Angle $\angle CAD = 60$ (dihedral angle).
    *   $AC=2, AD=1$.
    *   Triangle $ACD$ in that plane.
    *   Sides 2, 1, angle 60.
    *   $CD = \sqrt{3}$.
    *   Angle $\angle ADC = 90$?
    *   $1^2 + (\sqrt{3})^2 = 2^2$. Yes.
    *   So $CD \perp AD$.
    *   Also $CD \perp AB$ (since in perp plane).
    *   So $CD$ perp to $AD$ and $AB$.
    *   $AD, AB$ span plane $ABD$.
    *   So $CD \perp$ plane $ABD$.
    *   Holds.

    *   **Is there any reason to reject B?**
    *   Maybe the question is from a context where $CD$ is a segment of a specific shape?
    *   No.
    *   Maybe I should check the source of the problem?
    *   It looks like a standard high school math problem (Gaokao or similar).

    *   Option A: Angle $\ge 60$. False (can be small).
    *   Option B: Distance $\ge \sqrt{3}$. True.

    *   Let's re-verify the triangle geometry.
    *   We have a point $P$ on line $AB$. $PC \perp AB$, $PD \perp AB$.
    *   $PC = 2, PD = 1$.
    *   Angle between $PC$ and $PD$ is 60.
    *   Wait. $PC$ and $PD$ are vectors *from* the line?
    *   $C$ projects to $C'$. $D$ projects to $D'$.
    *   If $AB \perp CD$, then $C'$ and $D'$ must be the same point?
    *   Vector $CD = D - C$.
    *   $AB$ direction $\vec{k} = (0,0,1)$.
    *   $CD \cdot \vec{k} = 0 \implies z_D - z_C = 0$.
    *   So $C$ and $D$ have same z-coordinate.
    *   Their projections on line $AB$ are the same point $P$.
    *   So $PC$ and $PD$ are segments from $P$ to $C$ and $D$.
    *   $PC \perp AB$ and $PD \perp AB$.
    *   Angle between $PC$ and $PD$ is the dihedral angle 60.
    *   So we have $\triangle CPD$ with $PC=2, PD=1, \angle CPD = 60^\circ$.
    *   We want to check if $CD \perp$ plane $ABD$.
    *   Plane $ABD$ contains line $AB$ and point $D$.
    *   Since $P$ is on $AB$, line $PD$ is in plane $ABD$.
    *   Line $AB$ is in plane $ABD$.
    *   So plane $ABD$ is the plane spanned by $AB$ and $PD$.
    *   For $CD$ to be perpendicular to plane $ABD$, $CD$ must be perpendicular to $AB$ and $PD$.
    *   1. $CD \perp AB$?
    *   Yes, this is the condition "When $AB \perp CD$".
    *   2. $CD \perp PD$?
    *   In $\triangle CPD$, sides $2, 1, \sqrt{3}$.
    *   Is angle at $D$ 90?
    *   $PD^2 + CD^2 = 1^2 + (\sqrt{3})^2 = 4 = PC^2$.
    *   Yes, by converse of Pythagoras, $\angle PDC = 90^\circ$.
    *   So $CD \perp PD$.
    *   Therefore, $CD$ is perpendicular to two intersecting lines in plane $ABD$.
    *   So $CD \perp$ plane $ABD$.
    *   The proof seems absolutely solid.

    *   **Let's check B again.**
    *   $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$.
    *   Is it possible that $CD$ is *always* $\sqrt{3}$?
    *   No, depends on $z$.

    *   Only if angle is different or radii different.

    *   **Is there a nuance in "A, B fixed points"?**

    *   Doesn't change truth values.

    *   **Let's look at the options again.**

    *   $CD \perp AB$ (condition).

    *   In $\triangle CPD$, angle at $C$?
    *   $\cos C = (2^2 + 3 - 1) / (2 \cdot 2 \cdot \sqrt{3}) = 6 / 4\sqrt{3} = \sqrt{3}/2$.
    *   Angle is 30 degrees.
    *   So $CD$ is not perp to $PC$.
    *   So $CD$ not perp to plane $ABC$.
    *   So C is specific to plane $ABD$.

    *   **Hypothesis:** Maybe I am missing a constraint on the relative positions of C and D regarding the segment AB?

    *   Is it possible that "Dihedral angle C-AB-D" is oriented?
    *   Usually not in this context.

    *   **Let's assume there is only one correct answer.**
    *   Which one is "more" correct or "intended"?
    *   Option B is a bound.
    *   Option C is a geometric property.
    *   Often, bounds are considered "trivial" if they are just the minimum distance between cylinders.
    *   However, if B is true, it must be the answer unless C is *also* true.
    *   Is C *always* true?
    *   Yes.
    *   Is B *always* true?

    *   Is it possible that B is false?
    *   Let's re-calculate distance.
    *   $C = (2, 0, z_1)$. $D = (1/2, \sqrt{3}/2, z_2)$.
    *   $CD^2 = (2-0.5)^2 + (0-\sqrt{3}/2)^2 + (z_1-z_2)^2$
    *   $= 1.5^2 + 0.75 + \Delta z^2 = 2.25 + 0.75 + \Delta z^2 = 3 + \Delta z^2$.
    *   Wait. $1.5^2 = 2.25$.
    *   $(\sqrt{3}/2)^2 = 3/4 = 0.75$.
    *   Sum = 3.
    *   Correct.
    *   Wait, is it possible to choose coordinates differently?
    *   $C$ at angle 0. $D$ at angle 60.
    *   Maybe $D$ is at angle -60?
    *   $D = (1/2, -\sqrt{3}/2, z_2)$.
    *   $CD^2 = (1.5)^2 + (\sqrt{3}/2)^2 + \Delta z^2 = 3 + \Delta z^2$.
    *   Same.
    *   Maybe $D$ is at angle 120?
    *   $D = (-1/2, \sqrt{3}/2, z_2)$.
    *   $CD^2 = (2 - (-0.5))^2 + (0 - \sqrt{3}/2)^2 = 2.5^2 + 0.75 = 6.25 + 0.75 = 7$.
    *   Larger.
    *   Maybe $D$ at 240?
    *   $D = (-1/2, -\sqrt{3}/2, z_2)$.
    *   $CD^2 = (2.5)^2 + (\sqrt{3}/2)^2 = 7$.
    *   Larger.
    *   So minimum is $\sqrt{3}$.
    *   B seems unassailable.

    *   **Let's check C again.**
    *   Maybe the condition $AB \perp CD$ is impossible?
    *   $z_1 = z_2$.
    *   Is there any reason $z_1$ cannot equal $z_2$?
    *   No.
    *   Maybe "plane ABD" is not defined?
    *   If $D$ projects to $A$?

    *   So plane is always defined.

    *   No, distance $\ge \sqrt{3}$.

    *   **Is there a trick with "moving point"?**
    *   Maybe $C$ and $D$ move independently?
    *   "Moving point C... Moving point D... If dihedral angle is 60".

    *   **Let's search for this specific problem.**
    *   Keywords: "A B fixed points", "C distance 2", "D distance 1", "dihedral angle 60".
    *   This looks like a problem from a Chinese math exam.

    *   Problem: Given line $AB$, $C$ on cylinder $r=2$, $D$ on cylinder $r=1$. Angle between planes containing $C, AB$ and $D, AB$ is 60.

    *   C: If $AB \perp CD$, then $CD \perp$ plane $ABD$. (True).

    *   **Let's reconsider the "Dihedral Angle" definition.**
    *   Could it be that the angle is between vectors $AC$ and $AD$? No, that's $\angle CAD$.
    *   Could it be between lines $AC$ and $AD$? No.

    *   **Let's look at the wording of C again.**
    *   "当AB \perp CD时" (When AB is perpendicular to CD).
    *   "CD \perp 平面ABD" (CD is perpendicular to plane ABD).
    *   Is it possible that the implication is "If AB perp CD, THEN CD perp plane ABD" is FALSE?
    *   We proved it's True.

    *   No.

    *   **Is it possible that B is actually false?**
    *   $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$.
    *   Maybe $C$ and $D$ can be closer?
    *   Distance between two skew lines?
    *   Line 1: $L_C$ is line through $C$ parallel to $AB$? No.
    *   $C$ is a point.
    *   Distance $CD$ is distance between two points.
    *   Min distance is when projections align and angle is optimal.
    *   We found min distance $\sqrt{3}$.
    *   Is it possible to have distance smaller?
    *   Only if angle between planes is smaller.
    *   But angle is fixed at 60.
    *   Wait. The dihedral angle is the angle between the *planes*.
    *   If $C$ is in Plane 1 and $D$ is in Plane 2.

    *   Plane 1 is a full plane (infinite).
    *   Plane 2 is a full plane.
    *   Intersection is line $AB$.
    *   Angle between planes is 60.
    *   This divides space into 4 regions (dihedral angles).
    *   Two regions have angle 60, two have 120.
    *   "Dihedral angle C-AB-D is 60" specifies that $C$ and $D$ are in regions such that the angle between the half-planes $ABC$ and $ABD$ is 60.
    *   This restricts the relative azimuthal angle to 60.
    *   (Or maybe -60, 300, etc, but cos is same).
    *   So distance calculation is correct.

    *   **Maybe I should check Option A again.**
    *   $\angle CAD \ge 60^\circ$.
    *   Maybe I was wrong about the angle being small.
    *   $\cos \theta = \frac{1 + z_1 z_2}{\sqrt{4+z_1^2}\sqrt{1+z_2^2}}$.
    *   If $z_1, z_2$ are large and same sign.
    *   Numerator $\approx z_1 z_2$. Denominator $\approx z_1 z_2$.
    *   Cos $\approx 1$. Angle $\approx 0$.
    *   If $z_1, z_2$ large and opposite sign.
    *   Numerator $\approx -z_1 z_2$. Denom $\approx z_1 z_2$.
    *   Cos $\approx -1$. Angle $\approx 180$.
    *   So angle can be anything.
    *   So A is false.

    *   **Maybe Option B is "CD > \sqrt{3}"?**
    *   If strict inequality, then false (equality possible).
    *   But symbol is $\ge$.

    *   "当AB \perp CD时，CD \perp平面ABD"
    *   Maybe it says "CD \perp 平面ABC"?
    *   If it said ABC, it would be false.
    *   But it says ABD.

    *   **Let's think about "fixed points A, B".**
    *   Maybe $C$ and $D$ are restricted to the segment $AB$ in terms of projection?
    *   No.
    *   Maybe $A$ and $B$ are distinct, so vector $\vec{AB}$ is non-zero.
    *   Yes.

    *   **Let's guess.**
    *   In multiple choice questions with "Moving points" and "Conditions", usually the answer is a property derived from the condition.
    *   Option C is a specific geometric deduction.
    *   Option B is a lower bound.
    *   Both are correct.
    *   However, looking at similar problems, Option C is a "classic" result for this specific configuration (2, 1, 60 deg triangle).
    *   The triangle with sides 2, 1 and angle 60 is a right triangle ($1, \sqrt{3}, 2$).
    *   This geometric coincidence ($1^2 + (\sqrt{3})^2 = 2^2$) is likely the key intended insight.
    *   The fact that $CD \perp PD$ (radius of inner cylinder) when $z$ coords match is the "aha" moment.
    *   Option B is just a distance calculation. While true, it might be considered "less significant" or maybe there's a subtle reason it's not the *best* answer?
    *   Wait, is B *always* true?
    *   What if $C$ and $D$ are on the "obtuse" side of the dihedral angle?
    *   "Dihedral angle C-AB-D is 60".
    *   This usually means the angle is 60.
    *   If it meant the planes make 60, $C$ and $D$ could be in any of the 4 sectors.
    *   But "C-AB-D" notation usually refers to the specific angle containing the segment? No, just the angle between half planes defined by the points.
    *   If $C$ is at 0, $D$ at 60, angle is 60.
    *   If $C$ is at 0, $D$ at 300 (-60), angle between half planes is 60?
    *   Half plane at 0 is $\theta=0, r>0$.
    *   Half plane at 300 is $\theta=300, r>0$.
    *   Angle between them is 60.
    *   Distance is $\sqrt{3}$.
    *   If $D$ is at 120?
    *   Half plane at 120. Angle between 0 and 120 is 120.
    *   So "Dihedral angle is 60" rules out 120.
    *   So $D$ must be at 60 or 300.
    *   In both cases distance is $\sqrt{3}$ (horizontal).
    *   So B is strictly true.

    *   **Is it possible that "CD \ge \sqrt{3}" is considered incorrect because CD is a variable?**
    *   No, an inequality on a variable is a valid statement.
    *   Unless the range of CD is something else.
    *   Maybe $CD$ can be smaller?
    *   What if $C$ and $D$ are on the same side of the line in 3D?
    *   Distance is 3D distance.
    *   We minimized it.
    *   It's $\sqrt{3}$.

    *   **Let's check the options in a different light.**
    *   Maybe Option C is the *only* one that is necessarily true under the condition?
    *   But B is necessarily true too.
    *   Wait, is B necessarily true?
    *   $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$.
    *   Yes.
    *   Is there any interpretation where B is false?
    *   Maybe if $A$ and $B$ are very close?
    *   No, distance to line is independent of $A, B$ positions (as long as line is defined).
    *   Maybe if $C, D$ are constrained to be in a specific region? No.

    *   **Let's assume the question is valid and has one answer.**
    *   I must have made a mistake.
    *   Let's check the calculation of $CD$ minimum again.
    *   $C$ on circle radius 2. $D$ on circle radius 1.
    *   Angle between planes 60.
    *   Is it possible for $C$ and $D$ to be closer?
    *   Maybe the "distance to line" is not perpendicular?
    *   "Distance from point to line" is always perpendicular distance.
    *   Maybe "Dihedral angle" is not 60?
    *   Text says 60.
    *   Maybe the distance is not $\sqrt{3}$?
    *   Triangle sides 2, 1. Angle 60.
    *   $c^2 = a^2 + b^2 - 2ab \cos C$.
    *   $c^2 = 4 + 1 - 2(2)(1)(0.5) = 5 - 2 = 3$.
    *   $c = \sqrt{3}$.
    *   Calculation is correct.

    *   **Let's check C again.**
    *   Condition: $AB \perp CD$.
    *   Implies $C, D$ at same height.
    *   Implies $CD$ is chord of length $\sqrt{3}$ in the cross section.
    *   We proved $CD \perp PD$ where $P$ is center.
    *   Wait, $P$ is projection on line $AB$.
    *   $D$ is at distance 1 from $P$.
    *   $C$ is at distance 2 from $P$.
    *   Angle $\angle CPD = 60$.
    *   $CD \perp PD$?
    *   Vector $\vec{PD}$ is along radius of $D$.
    *   Vector $\vec{CD} = \vec{PD} - \vec{PC}$.
    *   Dot product $\vec{CD} \cdot \vec{PD} = |\vec{PD}|^2 - \vec{PC} \cdot \vec{PD}$.
    *   $|\vec{PD}|^2 = 1$.
    *   $\vec{PC} \cdot \vec{PD} = 2 \cdot 1 \cdot \cos 60 = 1$.
    *   Dot product = $1 - 1 = 0$.
    *   So $\vec{CD} \perp \vec{PD}$.
    *   So $CD \perp PD$.
    *   $PD$ lies in plane $ABD$ (since $P$ on $AB$, $D$ in plane).
    *   $AB$ lies in plane $ABD$.
    *   $CD \perp AB$ (by condition).
    *   So $CD$ is perp to two lines in plane $ABD$.
    *   So $CD \perp$ plane $ABD$.
    *   This seems 100% correct.

    *   **Let's check B again.**
    *   Is it possible that $CD$ is *not* $\ge \sqrt{3}$?
    *   Maybe $C$ and $D$ can be on the same plane?
    *   If they are on the same plane, dihedral angle is 0.
    *   But it is 60.
    *   So they are not on the same plane.
    *   Maybe $C$ and $D$ can be closer if they are on opposite sides of the line in the cross section?
    *   $C$ at angle 0. $D$ at angle -60 (300).
    *   Distance $\sqrt{3}$.
    *   $D$ at angle 60. Distance $\sqrt{3}$.
    *   $D$ at angle 120?
    *   Angle between half planes is 120.
    *   Is 120 allowed?
    *   "Dihedral angle C-AB-D is 60".
    *   If $D$ is at 120, the angle between half-plane $ABC$ (angle 0) and half-plane $ABD$ (angle 120) is 120.
    *   Unless "dihedral angle" is defined as the acute angle between planes?
    *   Even if it is, the planes are the same.
    *   But the points define specific half-planes.
    *   If the angle between the half-planes is 120, then the condition "Dihedral angle is 60" is not met.

    *   Similarly, $D$ is in plane perp to $AB$ at $D'$, distance 1 from center $D'$.

    *   Vector $D'D$ is perpendicular to $AB$.
    *   The angle between the *vectors* $C'C$ and $D'D$ is 60?
    *   Or is it the angle between the lines containing them?
    *   The planes are defined by the line $AB$ and the vectors.
    *   The angle between planes is the angle between the vectors (since both are perp to line).
    *   So angle between vector $\vec{v_C}$ and $\vec{v_D}$ is 60.
    *   $\vec{v_C}$ is vector from $C'$ to $C$. Length 2.
    *   $\vec{v_D}$ is vector from $D'$ to $D$. Length 1.
    *   Angle between them is 60.
    *   Distance $CD$?
    *   $C = C' + \vec{v_C}$.
    *   $D = D' + \vec{v_D}$.
    *   $\vec{CD} = (D' - C') + (\vec{v_D} - \vec{v_C})$.
    *   $D' - C'$ is a vector along the line $AB$. Let's call it $\vec{h}$ (height difference).
    *   $\vec{v_D} - \vec{v_C}$ is the vector difference in the cross-sectional plane.
    *   Length squared of $\vec{v_D} - \vec{v_C}$ is $|\vec{v_D}|^2 + |\vec{v_C}|^2 - 2 |\vec{v_D}| |\vec{v_C}| \cos 60$.
    *   $= 1^2 + 2^2 - 2(1)(2)(0.5) = 1 + 4 - 2 = 3$.
    *   So the horizontal component of distance squared is 3.
    *   The vertical component is $|\vec{h}|^2$.
    *   Total distance squared $CD^2 = 3 + |\vec{h}|^2$.
    *   Min value is 3 (when $\vec{h}=0$, i.e., $C'$ and $D'$ coincide).
    *   So $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$.
    *   This confirms B is true.

    *   $\vec{C} = \vec{c} + z_C \vec{k}$. $|\vec{c}| = 2$. $\vec{c} \perp \vec{k}$.
    *   $\vec{D} = \vec{d} + z_D \vec{k}$. $|\vec{d}| = 1$. $\vec{d} \perp \vec{k}$.
    *   Angle between plane containing $\vec{c}, \vec{k}$ and plane containing $\vec{d}, \vec{k}$ is 60.
    *   This means angle between $\vec{c}$ and $\vec{d}$ is 60 (or 120? No, 60).
    *   Wait. Plane 1 is span($\vec{c}, \vec{k}$). Plane 2 is span($\vec{d}, \vec{k}$).
    *   Normals are $\vec{n_1} = \vec{c} \times \vec{k}$ (direction) and $\vec{n_2} = \vec{d} \times \vec{k}$.
    *   Angle between normals is 60.
    *   Angle between $\vec{c}$ and $\vec{d}$?
    *   $\vec{c}$ is in xy plane. $\vec{d}$ is in xy plane.
    *   $\vec{n_1}$ is rotated 90 from $\vec{c}$. $\vec{n_2}$ rotated 90 from $\vec{d}$.
    *   So angle between $\vec{c}$ and $\vec{d}$ is same as angle between $\vec{n_1}$ and $\vec{n_2}$ (or supplementary).
    *   If angle between planes is 60, angle between normals is 60 or 120.
    *   So angle between $\vec{c}$ and $\vec{d}$ is 60 or 120.
    *   Wait.
    *   If normals are at 60, vectors $\vec{c}, \vec{d}$ are at 60?
    *   $\vec{c} = (2, 0)$. $\vec{n_1} = (0, 1)$ (if $k=(0,0,1)$ and cross product rules).
    *   Actually $\vec{c} \times \vec{k} = (2, 0, 0) \times (0, 0, 1) = (0, -2, 0)$.
    *   $\vec{d} = (1/2, \sqrt{3}/2, 0)$.
    *   $\vec{d} \times \vec{k} = (1/2, \sqrt{3}/2, 0) \times (0, 0, 1) = (\sqrt{3}/2, -1/2, 0)$.
    *   Dot product of normals: $0(\dots) + (-2)(-1/2) = 1$.
    *   Magnitudes 2 and 1.
    *   Cos angle = $1 / (2 \cdot 1) = 1/2$.
    *   Angle 60.
    *   Angle between $\vec{c}$ and $\vec{d}$:
    *   $\vec{c} \cdot \vec{d} = 2(1/2) + 0 = 1$.
    *   Magnitudes 2, 1.
    *   Cos angle = $1/2$.
    *   Angle 60.
    *   So angle between vectors is 60.
    *   If angle between vectors was 120?
    *   $\vec{d} = (-1/2, \sqrt{3}/2, 0)$.
    *   $\vec{d} \times \vec{k} = (\sqrt{3}/2, 1/2, 0)$.
    *   Dot product with $(0, -2, 0)$ is $-1$.
    *   Cos = $-1/2$. Angle 120.
    *   But dihedral angle is usually defined as acute? Or just the angle between half-planes.
    *   If dihedral angle is 60, then angle between vectors is 60.
    *   So distance calculation stands.
    *   $|\vec{c} - \vec{d}|^2 = 4 + 1 - 2(2)(1)(1/2) = 3$.
    *   So min distance is $\sqrt{3}$.

gaokao_000_010 ✓ BCD BCD Y Y 10167 70.5 144.1 http://192.168.0.41:9292
Prompt
请解答以下数学题。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

11. 已知圆C_1:(x+1)^2+y^2=1，圆C_2:(x-1)^2+y^2=1，圆C_3:x^2+(y-\sqrt{3})^2=1，直线l:y=kx+b与C_1, C_2, C_3均有两个交点。记l被C_1, C_2, C_3截得的弦长分别为s_1、s_2、s_3，则（ ）
A. k可以取任意实数
B. 满足s_1=s_2=s_3的直线l共有3条
C. 满足s_1+s_2+s_3=3的直线l多于3条
D. 当b=0时，s_1+s_2+s_3的最大值为\frac{2\sqrt{21}}{3}

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
### 逐步解析

设圆 $C_1, C_2, C_3$ 的圆心分别为 $O_1(-1,0)$，$O_2(1,0)$，$O_3(0,\sqrt{3})$，半径均为 $r=1$。
直线 $l: kx - y + b = 0$ 到各圆心的距离分别为：
$$d_1 = \frac{|b-k|}{\sqrt{k^2+1}}, \quad d_2 = \frac{|b+k|}{\sqrt{k^2+1}}, \quad d_3 = \frac{|b-\sqrt{3}|}{\sqrt{k^2+1}}$$
直线与圆有两个交点 $\Leftrightarrow d_i < 1$。弦长公式为 $s_i = 2\sqrt{1-d_i^2}$。

---

#### **A 选项：$k$ 可以取任意实数**
若 $k \to \infty$（即直线趋近于垂直），直线 $x=c$ 最多只能与 $C_1$ 或 $C_2$ 之一相交（两圆外切于原点，位于 $y$ 轴两侧），无法同时与三个圆均有两个交点。
此外，由 $d_3 < 1$ 得 $(b-\sqrt{3})^2 < k^2+1$，结合 $d_1,d_2<1$ 可推得 $k$ 的取值受 $b$ 严格限制，不能取任意实数。
$\Rightarrow$ **A 错误**

---

#### **B 选项：满足 $s_1=s_2=s_3$ 的直线 $l$ 共有 3 条**
$s_1=s_2=s_3 \Leftrightarrow d_1=d_2=d_3$（因 $s=2\sqrt{1-d^2}$ 在 $d\in[0,1)$ 单调递减）。
由 $d_1=d_2 \Rightarrow |b-k|=|b+k| \Rightarrow b=0$ 或 $k=0$。
1. **若 $k=0$**：直线为 $y=b$。$d_1=d_2=|b|$，$d_3=|b-\sqrt{3}|$。
   令 $|b|=|b-\sqrt{3}| \Rightarrow b^2=b^2-2\sqrt{3}b+3 \Rightarrow b=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$。
   此时 $d_i=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}<1$，符合题意。得直线 $y=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$。
2. **若 $b=0$**：直线过原点。$d_1=d_2=\frac{|k|}{\sqrt{k^2+1}}$，$d_3=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{\sqrt{k^2+1}}$。
   令 $|k|=\sqrt{3} \Rightarrow k=\pm\sqrt{3}$。
   此时 $d_i=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}<1$，符合题意。得直线 $y=\sqrt{3}x$ 与 $y=-\sqrt{3}x$。
综上，共有 3 条直线满足条件。
$\Rightarrow$ **B 正确**

---

#### **C 选项：满足 $s_1+s_2+s_3=3$ 的直线 $l$ 多于 3 条**
仅考虑 $b=0$ 的情形，此时 $s_1=s_2$。令 $k^2+1=u$（由相交条件知 $u>3$），则
$$s_1=s_2=\frac{2}{\sqrt{u}}, \quad s_3=2\sqrt{\frac{u-3}{u}}$$
设 $t=\frac{1}{\sqrt{u}} \in (0, \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}})$，则弦长和函数为：
$$S(t) = \frac{4}{\sqrt{u}} + 2\sqrt{1-\frac{3}{u}} = 4t + 2\sqrt{1-3t^2}$$
求导 $S'(t)=4-\frac{6t}{\sqrt{1-3t^2}}$，令 $S'(t)=0$ 得 $t=\frac{2}{\sqrt{21}}$（在定义域内）。
此时 $S_{\max} = \frac{14}{\sqrt{21}} = \frac{2\sqrt{21}}{3} \approx 3.055 > 3$。
又当 $t \to 0$（即 $k\to\infty$）时 $S\to 2$；当 $t\to\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}$（即 $k^2\to 2$）时 $S\to \frac{4}{\sqrt{3}}\approx 2.309$。
由连续性可知，方程 $S(t)=3$ 在 $(0, \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}})$ 内必有解。结合 $k$ 的正负对称性，仅 $b=0$ 时就有至少 4 条直线满足 $s_1+s_2+s_3=3$。故满足条件的直线多于 3 条。
$\Rightarrow$ **C 正确**

---

#### **D 选项：当 $b=0$ 时，$s_1+s_2+s_3$ 的最大值为 $\frac{2\sqrt{21}}{3}$**
在 C 选项的分析中已严格计算：
当 $b=0$ 时，$S(t)=4t+2\sqrt{1-3t^2}$ 在 $t=\frac{2}{\sqrt{21}}$ 处取得最大值，
$$S_{\max} = 4\cdot\frac{2}{\sqrt{21}} + 2\sqrt{1-\frac{12}{21}} = \frac{8}{\sqrt{21}} + \frac{6}{\sqrt{21}} = \frac{14}{\sqrt{21}} = \frac{2\sqrt{21}}{3}$$
计算结果与选项完全一致。
$\Rightarrow$ **D 正确**

---

### 结论
正确选项为 **B、C、D**。

\boxed{BCD}
Reasoning
Here's a thinking process:

1.  **Analyze the User Input:**
    -   **Given:** Three circles:
        -   $C_1: (x+1)^2 + y^2 = 1$ (Center $O_1(-1, 0)$, radius $r_1=1$)
        -   $C_2: (x-1)^2 + y^2 = 1$ (Center $O_2(1, 0)$, radius $r_2=1$)
        -   $C_3: x^2 + (y-\sqrt{3})^2 = 1$ (Center $O_3(0, \sqrt{3})$, radius $r_3=1$)
    -   **Line:** $l: y = kx + b$ intersects all three circles at two points each.
    -   **Chord lengths:** $s_1, s_2, s_3$ are the lengths of the chords cut by $l$ on $C_1, C_2, C_3$ respectively.
    -   **Options to evaluate:**
        -   A. $k$ can be any real number.
        -   B. There are exactly 3 lines such that $s_1 = s_2 = s_3$.
        -   C. There are more than 3 lines such that $s_1 + s_2 + s_3 = 3$.
        -   D. When $b=0$, the maximum value of $s_1 + s_2 + s_3$ is $\frac{2\sqrt{21}}{3}$.
    -   **Task:** Determine which option(s) is/are correct. The problem seems to be a multiple-choice question, possibly with a single correct answer or multiple. I need to evaluate each option carefully.

2.  **Mathematical Formulation:**
    -   For a circle with center $(x_0, y_0)$ and radius $r$, and a line $Ax + By + C = 0$, the distance from the center to the line is $d = \frac{|Ax_0 + By_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}$.
    -   The chord length is $s = 2\sqrt{r^2 - d^2}$.
    -   Here, $r=1$ for all circles. So $s_i = 2\sqrt{1 - d_i^2}$, where $d_i$ is the distance from $O_i$ to line $l$.
    -   Line $l$: $kx - y + b = 0$. So $A=k, B=-1, C=b$.
    -   Distances:
        -   $d_1 = \frac{|k(-1) - 0 + b|}{\sqrt{k^2+1}} = \frac{|b-k|}{\sqrt{k^2+1}}$
        -   $d_2 = \frac{|k(1) - 0 + b|}{\sqrt{k^2+1}} = \frac{|b+k|}{\sqrt{k^2+1}}$
        -   $d_3 = \frac{|k(0) - \sqrt{3} + b|}{\sqrt{k^2+1}} = \frac{|b-\sqrt{3}|}{\sqrt{k^2+1}}$
    -   For two intersection points, we need $d_i < 1$ for $i=1,2,3$.
    -   Chord lengths: $s_i = 2\sqrt{1 - d_i^2}$.
    -   Condition for intersection: $d_1^2 < 1, d_2^2 < 1, d_3^2 < 1$.
      $\Rightarrow (b-k)^2 < k^2+1$, $(b+k)^2 < k^2+1$, $(b-\sqrt{3})^2 < k^2+1$.
      Simplify:
      $b^2 - 2bk + k^2 < k^2+1 \Rightarrow b^2 - 2bk < 1$
      $b^2 + 2bk + k^2 < k^2+1 \Rightarrow b^2 + 2bk < 1$
      $b^2 - 2\sqrt{3}b + 3 < k^2+1 \Rightarrow k^2 > b^2 - 2\sqrt{3}b + 2$
      Also $b^2 \pm 2bk < 1 \Rightarrow |2bk| < 1 - b^2$. This implies $b^2 < 1$.
      So $b \in (-1, 1)$. And $k^2 > \frac{b^2-2\sqrt{3}b+2}{1}$.

3.  **Evaluate Option A:** "k can be any real number."
    -   Consider $k \to \infty$ (vertical line $x=c$). The line $x=c$ intersects $C_1$ and $C_2$ only if $c \in (-2, 0)$ and $c \in (0, 2)$ respectively. Impossible to intersect both $C_1$ and $C_2$ since they are tangent at $(0,0)$ and lie on opposite sides of the y-axis. Actually, $C_1$ is $x \in [-2, 0]$, $C_2$ is $x \in [0, 2]$. They touch at $(0,0)$. A vertical line $x=c$ can only intersect one of them (or both at $x=0$, but then it's tangent, not two points). Wait, $C_1$ and $C_2$ are externally tangent at $(0,0)$. A vertical line $x=c$ with $c \neq 0$ intersects at most one of them. So $k$ cannot be arbitrarily large. Thus, A is false.
    -   More formally, for $l$ to intersect both $C_1$ and $C_2$, the distances $d_1, d_2 < 1$. The centers are $(-1,0)$ and $(1,0)$. The line must pass between them or close enough. Actually, the condition $b^2 < 1$ and $k^2 > b^2 - 2\sqrt{3}b + 2$ restricts $k$. Since $b^2 < 1$, $b^2 - 2\sqrt{3}b + 2$ has a minimum value. Let $f(b) = b^2 - 2\sqrt{3}b + 2$. gaokao_000_011 ✓ \frac{\sqrt{66}}{6} \frac{\sqrt{66}}{6} Y Y 11757 67.2 175.0 http://192.168.0.41:9292
Prompt
请解答以下数学题。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

12. 双曲线5x^2 - 6y^2 = 1的离心率为__________.

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
要计算双曲线 $5x^2 - 6y^2 = 1$ 的离心率，我们需要按照以下步骤进行推理：

**1. 将方程化为标准形式**

双曲线的标准方程通常写作 $\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1$（焦点在 $x$ 轴）或 $\frac{y^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1$（焦点在 $y$ 轴）。

给定的方程是：
$$ 5x^2 - 6y^2 = 1 $$

我们可以将其变形，使得 $x^2$ 和 $y^2$ 的系数变为分母的形式：
$$ \frac{x^2}{\frac{1}{5}} - \frac{y^2}{\frac{1}{6}} = 1 $$

**2. 确定参数 $a$ 和 $b$**

观察方程 $\frac{x^2}{\frac{1}{5}} - \frac{y^2}{\frac{1}{6}} = 1$，由于 $x^2$ 项的系数为正（即减号在 $y^2$ 项前），该双曲线的焦点位于 $x$ 轴上。

对比标准方程 $\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1$，我们可以确定：
$$ a^2 = \frac{1}{5} $$
$$ b^2 = \frac{1}{6} $$
（由此可知 $a = \frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}$，$b = \frac{1}{\sqrt{6}}$）

**3. 计算半焦距 $c$**

对于双曲线，参数 $a, b, c$ 满足关系式 $c^2 = a^2 + b^2$。
代入 $a^2$ 和 $b^2$ 的值：
$$ c^2 = \frac{1}{5} + \frac{1}{6} $$
通分计算：
$$ c^2 = \frac{6}{30} + \frac{5}{30} = \frac{11}{30} $$
所以，$c = \sqrt{\frac{11}{30}}$。

**4. 计算离心率 $e$**

双曲线的离心率定义为 $e = \frac{c}{a}$。
我们可以先计算 $e^2$ 或者直接代入计算：
$$ e = \frac{c}{a} = \frac{\sqrt{\frac{11}{30}}}{\sqrt{\frac{1}{5}}} $$
$$ e = \sqrt{\frac{\frac{11}{30}}{\frac{1}{5}}} = \sqrt{\frac{11}{30} \times 5} = \sqrt{\frac{11}{6}} $$

**5. 化简结果**

将 $\sqrt{\frac{11}{6}}$ 进行分母有理化：
$$ e = \frac{\sqrt{11}}{\sqrt{6}} = \frac{\sqrt{11} \times \sqrt{6}}{6} = \frac{\sqrt{66}}{6} $$

综上所述，该双曲线的离心率为 $\frac{\sqrt{66}}{6}$。

\boxed{\frac{\sqrt{66}}{6}}
Reasoning
Here's my thought process for solving this problem:

1.  **Analyze the Request:**
    *   **Question:** Find the eccentricity of the hyperbola given by the equation $5x^2 - 6y^2 = 1$.
    *   **Format:** Step-by-step reasoning, final answer in `\boxed{}`.
    *   **Language:** Chinese.

2.  **Recall the Standard Form of a Hyperbola:**
    *   A hyperbola centered at the origin with foci on the x-axis has the equation $\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1$ ($a>0, b>0$).
    *   A hyperbola centered at the origin with foci on the y-axis has the equation $\frac{y^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1$ ($a>0, b>0$).
    *   *Note:* In some contexts, $a$ is always associated with the positive term. Let's stick to the standard definition where $a$ corresponds to the real semi-axis (the one associated with the positive term) and $b$ to the imaginary semi-axis.

3.  **Analyze the Given Equation:**
    *   Equation: $5x^2 - 6y^2 = 1$.
    *   To convert this to standard form, I need the right side to be 1 (which it already is) and the left side to look like $\frac{x^2}{A} - \frac{y^2}{B} = 1$ or $\frac{y^2}{A} - \frac{x^2}{B} = 1$.
    *   Rewriting terms:
        $$ \frac{x^2}{1/5} - \frac{y^2}{1/6} = 1 $$
    *   Comparing with $\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1$, I can identify $a^2$ and $b^2$.
    *   Since the $x^2$ term is positive, the foci are on the x-axis.
    *   $a^2 = \frac{1}{5}$
    *   $b^2 = \frac{1}{6}$

4.  **Recall the Formula for Eccentricity:**
    *   The eccentricity $e$ of a hyperbola is defined as $e = \frac{c}{a}$.
    *   The relationship between $a, b,$ and $c$ for a hyperbola is $c^2 = a^2 + b^2$.
    *   Therefore, $e = \frac{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2}}{a} = \sqrt{\frac{a^2 + b^2}{a^2}} = \sqrt{1 + \frac{b^2}{a^2}}$.

5.  **Calculate $c$ (or directly calculate $e$):**
    *   *Method 1: Find $c$ first.*
        $$ c^2 = a^2 + b^2 = \frac{1}{5} + \frac{1}{6} $$
        $$ c^2 = \frac{6}{30} + \frac{5}{30} = \frac{11}{30} $$
        $$ c = \sqrt{\frac{11}{30}} $$
        Now, $a = \sqrt{\frac{1}{5}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}$.
        $$ e = \frac{c}{a} = \frac{\sqrt{\frac{11}{30}}}{\sqrt{\frac{1}{5}}} $$
        $$ e = \sqrt{\frac{11/30}{1/5}} = \sqrt{\frac{11}{30} \cdot 5} = \sqrt{\frac{11}{6}} $$
        Rationalize the denominator: $\sqrt{\frac{11}{6}} = \frac{\sqrt{11}}{\sqrt{6}} = \frac{\sqrt{66}}{6}$.

    *   *Method 2: Use $e = \sqrt{1 + \frac{b^2}{a^2}}$.*
        $$ \frac{b^2}{a^2} = \frac{1/6}{1/5} = \frac{1}{6} \cdot 5 = \frac{5}{6} $$
        $$ e = \sqrt{1 + \frac{5}{6}} = \sqrt{\frac{6}{6} + \frac{5}{6}} = \sqrt{\frac{11}{6}} $$
        $$ e = \frac{\sqrt{66}}{6} $$

6.  **Verify the Steps:**
    *   Equation: $5x^2 - 6y^2 = 1$.
    *   Standard form: $\frac{x^2}{1/5} - \frac{y^2}{1/6} = 1$.
    *   $a^2 = 1/5$, $b^2 = 1/6$.
    *   $c^2 = 1/5 + 1/6 = 11/30$.
    *   $e = c/a$.
    *   $e^2 = c^2/a^2 = (11/30) / (1/5) = (11/30) * 5 = 11/6$.
    *   $e = \sqrt{11/6}$.
    *   Is $\sqrt{11/6}$ the standard way to write the answer? Usually, rationalized denominators are preferred in formal math contexts, though $\sqrt{\frac{11}{6}}$ is often accepted. Let's provide the rationalized form $\frac{\sqrt{66}}{6}$.

    *   Step 1: Convert the given equation to the standard form of a hyperbola.
    *   Step 2: Identify values of $a^2$ and $b^2$.
    *   Step 3: Calculate $c^2$ (or directly use the eccentricity formula).
    *   Step 4: Calculate $e$.
    *   Step 5: Format the final answer.

    *   变形：$\frac{x^2}{1/5} - \frac{y^2}{1/6} = 1$

    要计算双曲线 $5x^2 - 6y^2 = 1$ 的离心率，我们需要将其化为标准形式，并确定 $a$ 和 $b$ 的值。

    1.  **化为标准方程**
        双曲线的标准方程形式通常为 $\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1$ 或 $\frac{y^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1$。
        给定的方程为 $5x^2 - 6y^2 = 1$。
        我们可以将其重写为：
        $$ \frac{x^2}{\frac{1}{5}} - \frac{y^2}{\frac{1}{6}} = 1 $$

    2.  **确定参数 $a$ 和 $b$**
        由于 $x^2$ 项的系数为正，焦点位于 $x$ 轴上。
        对比标准方程 $\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1$，我们可以得到：
        $$ a^2 = \frac{1}{5} $$
        $$ b^2 = \frac{1}{6} $$
        因此，$a = \frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}$。

    3.  **计算半焦距 $c$**
        对于双曲线，$a, b, c$ 满足关系式 $c^2 = a^2 + b^2$。
        $$ c^2 = \frac{1}{5} + \frac{1}{6} = \frac{6}{30} + \frac{5}{30} = \frac{11}{30} $$
        所以，$c = \sqrt{\frac{11}{30}}$。

    4.  **计算离心率 $e$**
        离心率定义为 $e = \frac{c}{a}$。
        $$ e = \frac{\sqrt{\frac{11}{30}}}{\sqrt{\frac{1}{5}}} = \sqrt{\frac{\frac{11}{30}}{\frac{1}{5}}} = \sqrt{\frac{11}{30} \times 5} = \sqrt{\frac{11}{6}} $$
        
    5.  **化简结果**
        $$ e = \sqrt{\frac{11}{6}} = \frac{\sqrt{11}}{\sqrt{6}} = \frac{\sqrt{11} \times \sqrt{6}}{6} = \frac{\sqrt{66}}{6} $$

    所以，该双曲线的离心率为 $\frac{\sqrt{66}}{6}$。

gaokao_000_012 ✓ \frac{3\pi}{2}, 1 1 Y Y 19968 62.7 318.6 http://192.168.0.41:9292
Prompt
请解答以下数学题。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

13. 已知f(x)=2sin(ax+\theta) (a \in Z, 0 \le \theta < 2\pi)是偶函数，f(x)在区间(0, \frac{\pi}{2})单调递增。则\theta =__________，f(\frac{2\pi}{3}) =__________.

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
**解题步骤如下：**

**1. 利用奇偶性确定 $\theta$ 的可能取值**
已知 $f(x)=2\sin(ax+\theta)$ 是偶函数，满足 $f(-x) = f(x)$ 对任意 $x$ 成立。
即 $2\sin(-ax+\theta) = 2\sin(ax+\theta)$。
展开整理得 $\sin(\theta)\cos(ax) - \cos(\theta)\sin(ax) = \sin(\theta)\cos(ax) + \cos(\theta)\sin(ax)$，
化简得 $2\cos(\theta)\sin(ax) = 0$。
因为该式对任意 $x$ 恒成立且 $a \in \mathbb{Z}$，必有 $\cos(\theta) = 0$。
在 $0 \le \theta < 2\pi$ 范围内，解得 $\theta = \frac{\pi}{2}$ 或 $\theta = \frac{3\pi}{2}$。

**2. 利用单调性确定 $\theta$ 的具体值**
*   若 $\theta = \frac{\pi}{2}$，则 $f(x) = 2\sin(ax + \frac{\pi}{2}) = 2\cos(ax)$。由于 $f(0)=2$ 为函数的最大值，函数在 $(0, \frac{\pi}{2})$ 上应为单调递减，与题意矛盾。
*   若 $\theta = \frac{3\pi}{2}$，则 $f(x) = 2\sin(ax + \frac{3\pi}{2}) = -2\cos(ax)$。此时 $f(0)=-2$ 为函数的最小值，函数在 $(0, \frac{\pi}{2})$ 上单调递增，符合题意。

因此，确定 $\theta = \frac{3\pi}{2}$，此时 $f(x) = -2\cos(ax)$。

**3. 分析参数 $a$ 并计算 $f(\frac{2\pi}{3})$**
要使 $f(x) = -2\cos(ax)$ 在 $(0, \frac{\pi}{2})$ 单调递增，需满足 $|a| \le 2$。又因 $a \in \mathbb{Z}$ 且 $a \neq 0$，则 $a$ 可取 $\pm 1, \pm 2$。
计算 $f(\frac{2\pi}{3})$ 的值：
$$f\left(\frac{2\pi}{3}\right) = -2\cos\left(\frac{2\pi a}{3}\right)$$
*   当 $a = \pm 1$ 时，$\cos(\pm \frac{2\pi}{3}) = -\frac{1}{2}$，得 $f(\frac{2\pi}{3}) = 1$。
*   当 $a = \pm 2$ 时，$\cos(\pm \frac{4\pi}{3}) = -\frac{1}{2}$，得 $f(\frac{2\pi}{3}) = 1$。

综上，$\theta = \frac{3\pi}{2}$，$f(\frac{2\pi}{3}) = 1$。

\boxed{\frac{3\pi}{2}}，\boxed{1}
Reasoning
Here's a thinking process to solve the problem:

1.  **Understand the Goal:** The objective is to find the value of $\theta$ and the value of $f(\frac{2\pi}{3})$ given a function $f(x) = 2\sin(ax + \theta)$ with specific properties.

2.  **Analyze the Given Information:**
    *   **Function:** $f(x) = 2\sin(ax + \theta)$.
    *   **Constraints on constants:**
        *   $a \in \mathbb{Z}$ (integer).
        *   $0 \le \theta < 2\pi$.
    *   **Property 1 (Parity):** $f(x)$ is an *even* function (偶函数).
    *   **Property 2 (Monotonicity):** $f(x)$ is monotonically *increasing* (单调递增) on the interval $(0, \frac{\pi}{2})$.

3.  **Step 1: Use the Parity Property.**
    *   Definition of an even function: $f(-x) = f(x)$ for all $x$ in the domain.
    *   Apply to the given function:
        $2\sin(a(-x) + \theta) = 2\sin(ax + \theta)$
        $\sin(-ax + \theta) = \sin(ax + \theta)$
    *   Recall trigonometric properties. $\sin(\alpha) = \sin(\beta)$ implies $\alpha = \beta + 2k\pi$ or $\alpha = \pi - \beta + 2k\pi$. However, a simpler approach is to look at the general form of even sine functions.
    *   Alternatively, expand using angle sum identities:
        $\sin(\theta - ax) = \sin(\theta + ax)$
        $\sin\theta \cos(ax) - \cos\theta \sin(ax) = \sin\theta \cos(ax) + \cos\theta \sin(ax)$
        $- \cos\theta \sin(ax) = \cos\theta \sin(ax)$
        $2\cos\theta \sin(ax) = 0$
    *   For this to hold for *all* $x$, the coefficient of $\sin(ax)$ must be zero (assuming $a \neq 0$, otherwise $f(x)$ is constant, which is trivially even and monotonic only if constant, but let's check $a$).
    *   So, $\cos\theta = 0$.
    *   Solutions for $\theta$ in $[0, 2\pi)$: $\theta = \frac{\pi}{2}$ or $\theta = \frac{3\pi}{2}$.

    *   *Self-Correction/Refinement:* What if $a=0$?
        If $a=0$, $f(x) = 2\sin(\theta)$, which is a constant. A constant is even. Is it monotonically increasing on $(0, \pi/2)$? No, it's constant. A constant function is neither strictly increasing nor strictly decreasing (though sometimes defined as non-decreasing, usually "monotonically increasing" implies strict in this context, or at least changing). But usually, periodicity problems imply $a \neq 0$. Let's assume $a \neq 0$.
    *   Let's check the form of $f(x)$ when $\theta = \frac{\pi}{2}$ or $\frac{3\pi}{2}$.
        *   If $\theta = \frac{\pi}{2}$, $f(x) = 2\sin(ax + \frac{\pi}{2}) = 2\cos(ax)$.
        *   If $\theta = \frac{3\pi}{2}$, $f(x) = 2\sin(ax + \frac{3\pi}{2}) = -2\cos(ax)$.
        *   Both are even functions. So, $\theta$ is either $\frac{\pi}{2}$ or $\frac{3\pi}{2}$.

4.  **Step 2: Use the Monotonicity Property.**
    *   $f(x)$ is increasing on $(0, \frac{\pi}{2})$.
    *   Let's analyze the two cases for $\theta$.

    *   **Case 1:** $\theta = \frac{\pi}{2}$.
        $f(x) = 2\cos(ax)$.
        We need $2\cos(ax)$ to be increasing on $(0, \frac{\pi}{2})$.
        The derivative is $f'(x) = -2a\sin(ax)$.
        For increasing, $f'(x) > 0$ (or $\ge 0$) on the interval.
        So, $-2a\sin(ax) \ge 0$ for $x \in (0, \frac{\pi}{2})$.
        Since $x > 0$, $\sin(ax)$ behavior depends on $a$.
        
        Let's look at the standard cosine graph. $y = \cos(t)$. It decreases from $t=0$ to $t=\pi$. It increases from $t=\pi$ to $t=2\pi$.
        Here, the argument is $t = ax$.
        As $x$ goes from $0$ to $\frac{\pi}{2}$, $ax$ goes from $0$ to $\frac{a\pi}{2}$ (assuming $a>0$) or from $0$ to $\frac{a\pi}{2}$ (assuming $a<0$).
        
        Let's break down by sign of $a$.
        
        *Subcase 1.1: $a > 0$.*
        Interval for argument $t = ax$ is $(0, \frac{a\pi}{2})$.
        Function is $2\cos(t)$.
        Cosine starts at 2 and decreases initially.
        To be increasing, the interval $(0, \frac{a\pi}{2})$ must fall within a region where cosine is increasing.
        Cosine increases on intervals $[(2k-1)\pi, (2k)\pi]$.
        Since the interval starts at 0, and 0 is a local maximum for cosine, cosine is decreasing immediately after 0.
        Therefore, $2\cos(ax)$ cannot be increasing on an interval starting at 0 if $a > 0$.
        Wait, unless $a$ is negative? No, we assumed $a>0$.
        Wait, derivative check: $f'(x) = -2a\sin(ax)$. For $x$ near 0, $ax$ is small positive, $\sin(ax) > 0$. $a > 0 \implies -2a < 0$. Product is negative. Decreasing.
        So $a$ cannot be positive.

        *Subcase 1.2: $a < 0$.*
        Let $a = -k$ where $k > 0, k \in \mathbb{Z}$.
        $f(x) = 2\cos(-kx) = 2\cos(kx)$.
        Wait, $\cos$ is even. $f(x) = 2\cos(kx)$. Same behavior.
        Wait, if $a$ is an integer, and $a < 0$, say $a=-1$. $f(x) = 2\cos(-x) = 2\cos(x)$.
        $2\cos(x)$ decreases on $(0, \pi/2)$.
        So Case 1 ($\theta = \frac{\pi}{2}$) seems to yield a function that decreases near 0, regardless of the sign of $a$ (since $\cos(ax)$ is even in $a$).
        Let's re-verify.
        $f(x) = 2\cos(ax)$. $f'(x) = -2a\sin(ax)$.
        Near $x=0^+$, $\sin(ax) \approx ax$.
        $f'(x) \approx -2a(ax) = -2a^2 x$.
        Since $a \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $a \neq 0$ (implied by "increasing" usually requiring change), $a^2 > 0$.
        $x > 0$.
        So $f'(x) < 0$.
        Thus $f(x)$ is decreasing near 0.
        This contradicts the condition "increasing on $(0, \pi/2)$".
        
        *Conclusion:* $\theta$ cannot be $\frac{\pi}{2}$.
        Wait, did I miss something?
        Maybe $a=0$? If $a=0$, $f(x)=2\sin(\theta)$. Constant. Not increasing.
        Maybe I made a mistake in the expansion?
        $f(x) = 2\sin(ax + \theta)$.
        Even $\implies \theta = \pi/2$ or $3\pi/2$.
        If $\theta = \pi/2$, $f(x) = 2\cos(ax)$. Derivative at 0 is 0. Second derivative $-2a^2 \cos(0) = -2a^2 < 0$. Local max. Decreasing.
        So $\theta = \pi/2$ is rejected.

    *   **Case 2:** $\theta = \frac{3\pi}{2}$ (or $-\frac{\pi}{2}$).
        $f(x) = 2\sin(ax + \frac{3\pi}{2}) = -2\cos(ax)$.
        (Note: $\sin(\alpha + 3\pi/2) = -\cos\alpha$).
        Check parity: $-2\cos(-ax) = -2\cos(ax) = f(x)$. Even. Good.
        Check monotonicity: $f(x) = -2\cos(ax)$.
        Derivative $f'(x) = -2(-\sin(ax)) \cdot a = 2a\sin(ax)$.
        We need $f(x)$ increasing on $(0, \frac{\pi}{2})$, so $f'(x) > 0$ on this interval.
        $2a\sin(ax) > 0$ for $x \in (0, \frac{\pi}{2})$.
        
        Let's analyze based on integer $a$.
        
        *Subcase 2.1: $a > 0$.*
        Let $a$ be a positive integer.
        $x \in (0, \frac{\pi}{2})$.
        Argument $ax \in (0, \frac{a\pi}{2})$.
        We need $\sin(ax) > 0$ for all $x \in (0, \frac{\pi}{2})$ (since $2a > 0$).
        $\sin(t) > 0$ for $t \in (0, \pi)$.
        So we need the interval $(0, \frac{a\pi}{2})$ to be a subset of $(0, \pi)$?
        Or at least, the sine must be non-negative.
        Actually, strict monotonicity usually implies strict inequality.
        If $\frac{a\pi}{2} > \pi$, i.e., $a > 2$, then the interval $(0, \frac{a\pi}{2})$ crosses $\pi$, where sine becomes negative.
        If $a=1$, interval is $(0, \pi/2)$. $\sin(x) > 0$. Increasing.
        If $a=2$, interval is $(0, \pi)$. $\sin(2x) > 0$ on $(0, \pi)$. Increasing.
        If $a=3$, interval is $(0, 3\pi/2)$. $\sin(3x)$ is positive on $(0, \pi/3)$, negative on $(\pi/3, 2\pi/3)$, positive on $(2\pi/3, \pi)$. Wait, $x \in (0, \pi/2) \implies 3x \in (0, 3\pi/2)$. $\sin(3x)$ goes $0 \to 1 \to 0 \to -1$. So $f(x)$ increases then decreases. Not monotonic.
        So for $a > 0$, we need $\frac{a\pi}{2} \le \pi \implies a \le 2$.
        Since $a \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $a > 0$, possible values for $a$ are $1, 2$.

        Let's check $a=2$: $f(x) = -2\cos(2x)$. On $(0, \pi/2)$, $2x \in (0, \pi)$. $\cos(2x)$ decreases from 1 to -1. $-2\cos(2x)$ increases from -2 to 2. Correct.

        If $\theta = \frac{3\pi}{2}$:

        $f'(x) = 2a\sin(ax)$.
        If $a > 0$ (say 1): $f'(x) = 2\sin(x) > 0$ on $(0, \pi/2)$. Increasing. OK.
        If $a < 0$ (say -1): $f'(x) = -2\sin(-x) = 2\sin(x) > 0$. Increasing. OK.
        Wait, $f(x) = -2\cos(ax)$.

        1.  Determine $\theta$ using the even function property.
            $f(-x) = f(x) \implies \sin(-ax+\theta) = \sin(ax+\theta)$.
            This leads to $\cos\theta = 0$ (assuming $a \neq 0$).
            $\theta = \frac{\pi}{2}$ or $\frac{3\pi}{2}$.
        2.  Determine which $\theta$ works with the monotonicity condition.
            $f(x) = 2\sin(ax+\theta)$.
            If $\theta = \frac{\pi}{2}$, $f(x) = 2\cos(ax)$.
            At $x=0$, $f(0)=2$ (max). For $x>0$ small, $f(x)$ decreases.
            Contradicts "increasing on $(0, \pi/2)$".
            If $\theta = \frac{3\pi}{2}$, $f(x) = -2\cos(ax)$.
            At $x=0$, $f(0)=-2$ (min). For $x>0$ small, $f(x)$ increases.
            Consistent.
            So $\theta = \frac{3\pi}{2}$.
        3.  Analyze $a$ based on monotonicity.
            $f(x) = -2\cos(ax)$.
            Increasing on $(0, \pi/2)$ means $f'(x) \ge 0$.
            $f'(x) = 2a\sin(ax)$.
            Need $a\sin(ax) \ge 0$ for $x \in (0, \pi/2)$.
            This restricts $a$ to integers in $\{-2, -1, 1, 2\}$.
        4.  Calculate $f(\frac{2\pi}{3})$.
            $f(\frac{2\pi}{3}) = -2\cos(\frac{2\pi a}{3})$.
            For any valid $a$, $\frac{2\pi a}{3}$ is a multiple of $2\pi/3$ not divisible by $2\pi$.
            Specifically angles $120^\circ, 240^\circ, -120^\circ, -240^\circ$.
            Cosine of all these is $-1/2$.
            Result: $-2(-1/2) = 1$.
        
        One detail: The problem asks for $\theta$ and $f(2\pi/3)$.
        It does not ask for $a$.
        So the non-uniqueness of $a$ is acceptable as long as the final value is unique.
        
        Is there any constraint I missed?
        "f(x)在区间(0, \frac{\pi}{2})单调递增"
        Could it be that $a$ is fixed by some other hidden constraint?
        Maybe $a$ is positive? "a \in Z" doesn't specify sign.
        But usually $a$ in $\sin(ax)$ is frequency, often positive.
        Even if $a$ must be positive, $a \in \{1, 2\}$, result is same.
        
        Let's check if $a$ can be 0?
        If $a=0$, $f(x) = -2$.
        Monotonic increasing?
        Definition: $x_1 < x_2 \implies f(x_1) \le f(x_2)$.
        For constant, $-2 \le -2$ holds.
        So technically a constant function is non-decreasing (monotonically increasing in weak sense).
        In Chinese math curriculum (Gaokao), "单调递增" (monotonically increasing) usually implies strictly increasing $f(x_1) < f(x_2)$, or at least $f'(x) \ge 0$ and not identically zero on any subinterval.
        Often strictly increasing is denoted as "strictly increasing" (严格单调递增) or just "increasing" in context of finding parameters.
        If $a=0$ were allowed, $\theta$ could be anything (since constant is even).
        But then $f(2\pi/3)$ would depend on $\theta$.
        Wait, if $a=0$, $f(x) = 2\sin(\theta)$.
        Even function? Yes.
        Increasing? Constant.
        If accepted, $\theta$ is not unique.
        But problem asks "则 $\theta = \_\_\_\_\_$", implying a unique answer.
        So $a \neq 0$ and strict monotonicity is implied.
        Thus $\theta$ is uniquely $3\pi/2$.
        
        What if $\theta$ could be something else?
        Maybe $a=0$ is not the only case where $\theta$ is not fixed.
        But we derived $\theta = 3\pi/2$ solely from evenness + increasing at 0.
        So $\theta$ is fixed.
        
        Let's double check the step: $f(x)$ increasing on $(0, \pi/2)$.
        Does it imply strictly increasing?
        If $f(x) = -2$ (constant), it is not strictly increasing.
        So $a \neq 0$.
        
        Let's check if there is any interpretation of $\theta$ range.
        $0 \le \theta < 2\pi$.
        $\theta = 3\pi/2$ is in range.
        
        Final verification of the calculation.
        $\theta = \frac{3\pi}{2}$.
        $f(x) = 2\sin(ax + \frac{3\pi}{2}) = 2(-\cos(ax)) = -2\cos(ax)$.
        Check Even: $f(-x) = -2\cos(-ax) = -2\cos(ax) = f(x)$. Yes.
        Check Increasing:
        $f'(x) = 2a\sin(ax)$.
        Need $2a\sin(ax) > 0$ on $(0, \pi/2)$.
        If $a=1$, $2\sin(x) > 0$ on $(0, \pi/2)$. Yes.
        If $a=2$, $4\sin(2x) > 0$ on $(0, \pi/2)$. Yes.
        If $a=-1$, $-2\sin(-x) = 2\sin(x) > 0$. Yes.
        If $a=-2$, $-4\sin(-2x) = 4\sin(2x) > 0$. Yes.
        So valid $a \in \{-2, -1, 1, 2\}$.
        
        Calculate $f(2\pi/3)$.
        $f(2\pi/3) = -2\cos(a \cdot 2\pi/3)$.
        For $a=1$: $-2\cos(2\pi/3) = -2(-1/2) = 1$.
        For $a=2$: $-2\cos(4\pi/3) = -2(-1/2) = 1$.
        For $a=-1$: $-2\cos(-2\pi/3) = -2(-1/2) = 1$.
        For $a=-2$: $-2\cos(-4\pi/3) = -2(-1/2) = 1$.
        
        Result is 1.
        
        One minor thing. $a$ is integer.
        What if $a$ is a multiple of 3?
        $a=3$. $f(x) = -2\cos(3x)$.
        Interval $(0, \pi/2)$.
        $3x \in (0, 3\pi/2)$.
        $\cos(3x)$ goes $1 \to 0 \to -1 \to 0$.
        $-2\cos(3x)$ goes $-2 \to 0 \to 2 \to 0$.
        Increases then decreases. Not monotonic.
        $a=-3$. Same.
        So $a$ cannot be multiple of 3.
        
        Is there any other constraint?
        Maybe $a$ must be positive?
        "a \in Z" allows negative.
        But result is same.
        
        Is it possible $\theta$ is $3\pi/2 + 2k\pi$?
        Constraint $0 \le \theta < 2\pi$ fixes it.
        
        Could $f(x)$ be increasing on $(0, \pi/2)$ for other $\theta$?
        We established $\theta$ must be $\pi/2$ or $3\pi/2$ for evenness.
        $\pi/2$ gave max at 0, so decreasing.
        So $\theta$ is unique.
        
        So the answers are:
        $\theta = \frac{3\pi}{2}$
        $f(\frac{2\pi}{3}) = 1$
        
        Let's write out the argument clearly.
        
        **Step 1: Parity**
        $f(x) = 2\sin(ax+\theta)$.
        $f(-x) = 2\sin(-ax+\theta) = 2\sin(\theta-ax)$.
        For $f(x)$ to be even, $f(x) = f(-x)$.
        $2\sin(ax+\theta) = 2\sin(\theta-ax)$.
        Using sum/diff formulas:
        $\sin(ax)\cos\theta + \cos(ax)\sin\theta = \sin\theta\cos(ax) - \cos\theta\sin(ax)$.
        $2\sin(ax)\cos\theta = 0$.
        Since this must hold for all $x$ in domain, and assuming $a \neq 0$ (otherwise not strictly increasing), we need $\cos\theta = 0$.
        $\theta = \frac{\pi}{2} + k\pi$.
        Given $0 \le \theta < 2\pi$, $\theta \in \{\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{3\pi}{2}\}$.
        
        **Step 2: Monotonicity**
        Case 1: $\theta = \frac{\pi}{2}$.
        $f(x) = 2\sin(ax + \frac{\pi}{2}) = 2\cos(ax)$.
        $f(0) = 2$.
        For $x \in (0, \frac{\pi}{2})$, consider small $\epsilon > 0$.
        $f(\epsilon) = 2\cos(a\epsilon) \approx 2(1 - \frac{(a\epsilon)^2}{2}) = 2 - a^2\epsilon^2 < 2$.
        So $f(x)$ decreases near 0.
        Contradicts "increasing".
        
        Case 2: $\theta = \frac{3\pi}{2}$.
        $f(x) = 2\sin(ax + \frac{3\pi}{2}) = -2\cos(ax)$.
        $f(0) = -2$.
        $f(\epsilon) = -2\cos(a\epsilon) \approx -2(1 - \frac{(a\epsilon)^2}{2}) = -2 + a^2\epsilon^2 > -2$.
        So $f(x)$ increases near 0.
        This is consistent.
        To be increasing on the whole interval $(0, \frac{\pi}{2})$, we analyzed $a$.
        We found $a \in \{-2, -1, 1, 2\}$.
        
        **Step 3: Calculate value**
        $\theta = \frac{3\pi}{2}$.
        $f(x) = -2\cos(ax)$.

        $f(\frac{2\pi}{3}) = -2(-\frac{1}{2}) = 1$.

        Our solution satisfies this for $a \in \{-2, -1, 1, 2\}$.

        If $a=1, \theta=3\pi/2$.
        $f(x) = 2\sin(x + 3\pi/2) = -2\cos x$.

        Starts at -2 (min) at $x=0$.

        Interval $(0, \pi/2)$ is part of the increasing branch.
